--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully before designing the FSMs.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering "good" items, increases by 1 for "bad" items
- We need robots to: go to the correct half of the source (one color), pick up items, return to nest (white floor), deliver
- The challenge is identifying which half is beneficial (BLUE or GREEN wall)
- Since we don't know which is good initially, we need to explore and exploit

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor: BLACK (source area), WHITE (nest), GRAY (transit)
- Wall colors: RED (nest side), BLUE or GREEN (source halves)
- Robot can carry items and delivering to nest either decreases or increases score

**Strategy:**
- Since we're minimizing, we want robots to deliver the CORRECT item type
- Robots should go toward one colored half (say BLUE), pick up, return to nest (go toward RED wall), deliver
- Need to avoid the wrong half

**State design (3-4 states):**
1. **Explore/Transit**: Move toward source (go away from RED, or explore)
2. **At source**: Pick up from preferred half (GoToColor BLUE or GREEN)
3. **Return**: Go toward nest (GoToColor RED wall)
4. **Deliver**: In white area, transition back

I'll create 14 diverse machines covering different strategies:
- Some go to BLUE half, return via RED
- Some go to GREEN half, return via RED
- Various topologies (2-4 states)
- Mix of exploration and exploitation

## Reasoning

**Mission recap:** Minimize score = deliver "good" items (one color half decreases score by 1). Robots need to:
1. Navigate to the correct source half (BLUE or GREEN walls)
2. Pick up item
3. Return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall)
4. Deliver

**Key insight:** We don't know a priori which half (BLUE=color 2 or GREEN=color 3) is beneficial. Both must be tested. The best FSMs will consistently go to ONE half and return to nest.

**Improved strategy:**
- State 0: Explore/move toward source (GoToColor BLUE or GREEN, or explore)
- State 1: At source (BLACK floor) - already have item, immediately return
- State 2: Return to nest (GoToColor RED=color 1)
- State 3 (optional): In nest (WHITE floor) - go back to source

The critical transition: when on BLACK floor → switch to return state. When on WHITE floor → switch to source-seeking state.

I'll create a balanced set targeting both BLUE and GREEN halves, with varied topologies.

FSM: --nstates 3 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 2 --cle0 6 --n0 2 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.95 --n0x1 2 --c0x1 2 --p0x1 0.95 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 4 --n1 2 --n1x0 2 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.95 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 0 --p1x1 0.5 --s2 0 --rwm2 10 --cle2 0 --n2 2 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 0 --p2x0 0.8 --n2x1 1 --c2x1 2 --p2x1 0.2

## Reasoning

- Robots need: find source → pick up from ONE half → return to nest → repeat
- Floor colors: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit
- Wall colors: RED=nest side (color 1), BLUE=source half (color 2), GREEN=source half (color 3)
- GoToColor RED (clr=1) navigates toward nest
- GoToColor BLUE (clr=2) or GREEN (clr=3) navigates toward respective source half

**Most promising pattern:** 
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE/GREEN (seeking source) → transition on BLACK floor → go to return state; transition on WHITE floor → stay or reset
- State 1: GoToColor RED (returning to nest) → transition on WHITE floor → go back to source; transition on BLACK floor (wrong, re-entered source) → handle

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I still haven't received explicit score feedback, which means I need to be more systematic. Let me reconsider the mission fundamentals.

**Critical re-examination:**
- One source half DECREASES score (good), the other INCREASES it (bad)
- We don't know which color (BLUE=2 or GREEN=3) is good - this varies per run
- A robot that goes to the WRONG half will INCREASE the score (bad)
- The simplest reliable strategy: go to ONE half consistently and return to nest

**What should definitely work:**
- The 2-state machine (GoToColor→BLACK transition→GoToColor RED) is the purest form
- Key: probability=1.0 on floor transitions to make them deterministic
- The BLUE vs GREEN question: since we don't know, we need BOTH in the population and let the optimizer select

**Topology that makes most sense:**
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (or GREEN) - seeking source
  - On BLACK floor (p=1.0) → State 1 (return)
  - On WHITE floor (p=1.0) → State 0 (already here, but need another state to reset - actually loop doesn't apply)
- State 1: GoToColor RED - returning to nest
  - On WHITE floor (p=1.0) → State 0 (go back)

The simplest 2-state is actually best. Let me heavily exploit this with both color variants plus some 3-state variants.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without score feedback, I need to think harder about what's actually happening. Let me reconsider fundamentally.

**Key insight I may have missed:** The problem says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." The robots don't know which is which at design time - but the FSM is fixed. So the FSM must commit to one color half. If BLUE is good, BLUE-targeting FSMs score well; if GREEN is good, GREEN-targeting ones do.

**Another critical issue:** GoToColor uses the *camera* to find colored *objects* (wall blocks). Color 1=RED, 2=BLUE, 3=GREEN. The nest-side wall is RED. The source halves have BLUE and GREEN wall blocks behind them.

**What I haven't tried enough:** Using GoAwayColor RED to move AWAY from nest toward source, combined with GoToColor RED to return. This is simpler and doesn't require knowing which source half is good - robots just go to the source (away from RED), pick up whatever they find, and return (toward RED). But they'd pick up random items from both halves, giving ~0 net score.

**The real solution:** Must target ONE specific half. The 2-state machine with deterministic transitions (p=1.0) should be the purest. Let me focus heavily on these with both color variants, and add GoAwayRED as exploration toward source.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach. The fact that no scores are being reported suggests either the machines are performing similarly, or I'm missing something structural.

**Deep re-examination of the mission:**
- Score is MINIMIZED. Delivering good items → score -1. Delivering bad items → score +1.
- The critical unknown: which wall color (BLUE=2 or GREEN=3) corresponds to the good half.
- Both BLUE and GREEN variants should be tried equally.

**What I've been doing:** GoToColor BLUE/GREEN to find source, then GoToColor RED to return. This is logically sound.

**Potential issues I haven't addressed:**
1. When a robot is in the nest (WHITE) and does GoToColor BLUE/GREEN, it might not see the colored walls yet and wander randomly - this is fine.
2. GoToColor RED while already at nest (WHITE) is wasteful - robot should immediately switch back to seeking source.
3. **Missing transition:** In the return state (GoToColor RED), if the robot accidentally enters the source (BLACK), it should switch back to return mode immediately - but it already has an item so this just replaces it with a (possibly wrong) item.

**Key improvement:** Add a transition from the return state back to source-seeking when WHITE floor is detected (delivered!), AND keep the BLACK→return transition in the source-seeking state. Also ensure the nest state doesn't linger.

Let me try a cleaner 3-state design:
- S0: GoToColor BLUE → BLACK triggers S1, WHITE triggers S2
- S1: GoToColor RED (carrying item, return) → WHITE triggers S0
- S2: GoToColor BLUE (in nest, go back) → BLACK triggers S1, GRAY triggers S0

Actually S0 and S2 are identical. The pure 2-state is best. Let me also try GoAwayColor RED as a source-finder.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 2 --cle0 6 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 4 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 1.0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 7 rounds without score feedback, I need to think about what might be going wrong fundamentally.

**Critical re-examination of color IDs:**
The behavior parameters say `--clr<I>` uses colors {1,2,3,4,5,6}. But what ARE these colors? The problem says LED colors are {0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan} for `--cle`. For `--clr` in GoToColor/GoAwayColor, the color IDs {1,2,3,4,5,6} correspond to perceived object colors.

The arena has: RED wall (nest side), BLUE wall blocks, GREEN wall blocks. In ARGoS, these likely map to color IDs: RED=1, GREEN=2 (or 3?), BLUE=3 (or 2?). I've been assuming RED=1, BLUE=2, GREEN=3 - but this might be wrong!

**Alternative mapping possibility:** Perhaps the camera color IDs don't directly map to wall colors as I assumed. The robots might not reliably see wall colors from far away.

**What I know works conceptually:**
- Floor sensing: BLACK=0, GRAY=1, WHITE=2 - these are reliable
- The floor colors tell us WHERE we are: source=BLACK, transit=GRAY, nest=WHITE

**Simpler approach that avoids color uncertainty:**
- Use GoAwayColor RED (clr=1) to move away from nest toward source
- Use GoToColor RED (clr=1) to return to nest
- Floor BLACK → switch to return; Floor WHITE → switch to explore

This avoids needing to know BLUE vs GREEN mapping. The robot goes to the source randomly (both halves), but that gives ~0 score. Not great.

**The real key:** We MUST target one specific half. Let me try ALL possible color values (1-6) for GoToColor in source-seeking, to find which one correctly targets a source half.

Also trying: use condition C=7 (perceive color) with various colors to detect when near the right wall, then stop/collect.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds without score feedback, I need to radically reconsider. The lack of feedback itself is informative - perhaps all my machines score similarly (around 0, or some positive value) because:

1. **Robots are going to BOTH halves randomly** - if GoToColor doesn't reliably bias toward one wall color, robots get ~equal items from both halves → net score ≈ 0
2. **The color IDs might not map to wall colors as I think** - maybe the camera sees wall blocks differently
3. **GoToColor might not distinguish source halves well** - the robot might just go toward the nearest colored object regardless

**Fundamental rethink:** What if I use the robot's LED colors to create stigmergy? Robots that find the good half signal to others. But we don't know which is good at design time.

**Alternative strategy - pure exploitation of floor sensing:**
- The source is BLACK floor. The nest is WHITE floor. 
- What if robots AVOID the source entirely? Score = 0 (no deliveries). 
- What if they only use one half? Depends on which is good.

**New idea - use C=7 (perceive color) conditions:**
- If a robot perceives BLUE walls (color 2), it's near the BLUE half
- If it perceives GREEN walls (color 3), it's near the GREEN half
- Use these to switch between "go to BLUE half" and "return" states

**Most important insight I've missed:** Maybe I should try machines where robots go to source via exploration (random walk away from nest), not GoToColor. The GoToColor behavior needs the target to be visible - if walls are far away or partially hidden, robots might not navigate correctly.

Let me try: Exploration toward source + floor-based transitions, with GoToColor RED for return only.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. After 9 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to make my best guesses and cover the design space thoroughly.

**What I've learned from the absence of feedback:**
- The optimizer is running but I'm not seeing which machines score best
- I've been consistently trying GoToColor BLUE/GREEN + GoToColor RED return
- I've tried exploration-based approaches
- I've tried all color values 1-6

**Final strategic thinking:**

The mission says score is MINIMIZED. If all robots deliver nothing, score=0. If they deliver good items, score goes negative (better). If bad items, positive (worse).

The key insight: **one color half is always good, one always bad per run**. The FSM is fixed across runs. So if BLUE is good 50% of runs and GREEN is good 50%, then:
- A BLUE-only robot averages: 50% × (-N) + 50% × (+N) = 0
- Same for GREEN-only

**BUT:** The optimizer picks the BEST machine. So if BLUE happens to be good in most evaluation runs, the BLUE-targeting machine wins. The optimizer averages over many runs though.

**Alternative interpretation:** Maybe the blue/green assignment is FIXED per optimization run (not random per simulation). In that case, the optimizer will find which color is good and reward that FSM.

**Most reliable approach for final round:**
1. Keep the pure 2-state machines (both BLUE and GREEN) - these are the cleanest
2. Add machines that use BOTH colors in different states (try BLUE, if that fails try GREEN)
3. Add exploration-based returns
4. Try using GoAwayColor on the bad-half color as avoidance

Let me include the most diverse and robust set possible for this final round.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
